=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given three integers m, n, and k.
There is a rectangular grid of size m × n containing k identical pieces. Return the sum of Manhattan distances between every pair of pieces over all valid arrangements of pieces.
A valid arrangement is a placement of all k pieces on the grid with at most one piece per cell.
Since the answer may be very large, return it modulo 10^9 + 7.
The Manhattan Distance between two cells (x_i, y_i) and (x_j, y_j) is |x_i - x_j| + |y_i - y_j|.
 
Example 1:

Input: m = 2, n = 2, k = 2
Output: 8
Explanation:
The valid arrangements of pieces on the board are:


In the first 4 arrangements, the Manhattan distance between the two pieces is 1.
In the last 2 arrangements, the Manhattan distance between the two pieces is 2.

Thus, the total Manhattan distance across all valid arrangements is 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 + 2 = 8.

Example 2:

Input: m = 1, n = 4, k = 3
Output: 20
Explanation:
The valid arrangements of pieces on the board are:


The first and last arrangements have a total Manhattan distance of 1 + 1 + 2 = 4.
The middle two arrangements have a total Manhattan distance of 1 + 2 + 3 = 6.

The total Manhattan distance between all pairs of pieces across all arrangements is 4 + 6 + 6 + 4 = 20.

 
Constraints:

1 <= m, n <= 10^5
2 <= m * n <= 10^5
2 <= k <= m * n

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        N = m * n
        # Precompute factorials up to N
        fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        for i in range(1, N + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD
        invfact = [1] * (N + 1)
        invfact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(N, 0, -1):
            invfact[i-1] = invfact[i] * i % MOD
        def C(a, b):
            if b < 0 or b > a:
                return 0
            return fact[a] * invfact[b] % MOD * invfact[a-b] % MOD

        inv6 = pow(6, MOD-2, MOD)
        total_dist = (N % MOD) * ((n * (m*m - 1) + m * (n*n - 1)) % MOD) % MOD
        total_dist = total_dist * inv6 % MOD
        ways = C(N-2, k-2)
        return total_dist * ways % MOD

class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 1_000_000_007
        N = m * n

        fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        for i in range(1, N + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

        inv_fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        inv_fact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(N, 0, -1):
            inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

        def comb(a, b):
            if b < 0 or b > a:
                return 0
            return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % MOD * inv_fact[a - b] % MOD

        inv6 = pow(6, MOD - 2, MOD)
        pair_dist_sum = (N % MOD) * ((n * (m * m - 1) + m * (n * n - 1)) % MOD) % MOD
        pair_dist_sum = pair_dist_sum * inv6 % MOD

        return pair_dist_sum * comb(N - 2, k - 2) % MOD

Let's think if there is any issue with "k identical pieces" and "valid arrangement" if pieces are identical but "between every pair of pieces" maybe if k=3, there are 3 pairs. Example 2: first arrangement {1,2,3} total distance 4 = 1+1+2. Good.

Now, maybe there is a hidden issue with "modulo 10^9 + 7" and "answer may be very large" but D
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 1_000_000_007
        N = m * n

        fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        for i in range(1, N + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

        inv_fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        inv_fact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(N, 0, -1):
            inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

        def comb(a, b):
            if b < 0 or b > a:
                return 0
            return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % MOD * inv_fact[a - b] % MOD

        inv6 = pow(6, MOD - 2, MOD)

        # Sum of Manhattan distances over all unordered pairs of cells.
        pair_dist_sum = (N % MOD) * (
            (n * (m * m - 1) + m * (n * n - 1)) % MOD
        ) % MOD
        pair_dist_sum = pair_dist_sum * inv6 % MOD

        # Each unordered pair of cells appears in C(N-2, k-2) arrangements.
        return pair_dist_sum * comb(N - 2, k - 2) % MOD
```
